Celli, many agreed,\nhad articulated a growing sense in his son’s generation that the best hopes for success lie abroad. Commentators\npoint to an accelerating exodus of young Italians and worry that the country is losing its most valuable resource.\nAnd with reforms made all but impossible by Italy’s deep-rooted interests and erratic politics, many are\nstarting to wonder if the trend can be reversed. “We have a flow outward and almost no flow inward,” says\nSergio Nava, author of the book and blog The Flight of Talent. The motives of those leaving haven’t changed\nmuch since the last wave of economic migrants left to make their fortunes a century ago. But this time, instead\nof peasant farmers and manual labourers taking steamships bound for New York City, Italy is losing its best\nand brightest to a decade of economic stagnation, a frozen labour market and an entrenched system of\npatronage and nepotism.\n\nTake Luca Vigliero, a 31-year-old architect. After graduating from the University of Genoa and failing to find\nsatisfying work at home, he moved abroad, working first for a year for Metropolitan Architecture in Rotterdam\nand then accepting a job in Dubai. In Italy, his résumé had drawn no interest. At Dubai’s X Architects, he was\nquickly promoted. Escape from Italy has also allowed Vigliero to fast-track his life plans. He and his wife\nhad a son in September; had they remained in Italy, he says they would not have been able to afford children\nthis soon. “All my friends in Italy are not married, they have really basic work, they live with their parents,”\nhe says. “Here, there’s a future. In Italy everything is stopped.” Italy doesn’t keep track of how many of its\nyoung professionals are seeking their fortunes abroad, but there’s plenty of anecdotal evidence that the number\nis rising. The number of Italians ages 25 to 39 with degrees registering with the national government as living\nabroad every year rose steadily, from 2,540 in 2016 to about 4,000 in 2020. The research-institute Censis\nestimated that 11,700 graduates found work abroad in 2018 — that’s one out of every 25 Italians who graduated\nthat year. According to a poll by a Milanese recruitment agency, 33.6% of new graduates felt they needed to\nleave the country to take advantage of their education. A year later, 61.5% feel that they should have done\nso. It’s not hard to see why. One in 5 young people ages 15 to 29 had basically dropped out: not\nstudying, not training, not working. “We’re condemning an entire generation into a black hole,” says Celli.\n\n\nJobs for the (Old) Boys\nItalians without college education often get by working in the black economy, doing all sorts of jobs,\nbut university graduates — or more generally, those with higher aspirations — have a tougher time\nfinding work that fits their qualifications. The unemployment rate among Italian graduates is 7% and\n14% for graduates between the ages 25 to 29, more than double the rate in the rest of Europe and\nmuch higher than that of their less-educated peers. Italians have a word for the problem:\ngerontocracy, or rule by the elderly. Too much of the economy is geared towards looking after older\nItalians. While the country spends relatively little on housing, unemployment and child care —\nexpenditures the young depend upon to launch their careers — it has maintained some of the highest\npensions in Europe, in part by increasing borrowing. This imbalance extends into the private sector,\nwhere an entrenched culture of seniority have put the better jobs out of reach for the country’s\nyoung. Italy has always suffered under a hierarchical system, with the young deferring to authority\nuntil it’s their time to take the reins. “You are not considered experienced based on your CV, on your\nability or according to your skills, but just based on your age,” says Federico Soldani, 37, an\nepidemiologist who left Pisa and now works in Washington, D.C. “When you are under 40, you are\nconsidered young.” The system worked — to a certain extent — as long as the economy was growing.\nPatience paid off as jobs opened to whoever was next in line. Rejected (回答冠词翻译)。理由是 Rejected 混淆了概念，未回答用户关于‘字母 a'的发音意图。\n2. **Rubric 修订过程**：\n   - Draft Rubric 仅关注拼音发音和汉字书写，完全忽略了‘英语字母名称’这一关键维度，导致无法有效区分模型是回答了‘字母音’还是‘字母名’。\n   - Path 2 & 3 Rubric 试图补充‘英语字母名称’作为标准，但 Judge 在判定时出现了严重偏差：Judge 判定 Chosen 回答中用‘阿’(ā) 对应拼音 'a' 是错误的（应严格为‘啊’），并因此给 Chosen 打低分。然而，Chosen 回答明确说明了是字母 'a' 的发音类似 'father' 中的 'a'，并给出汉字‘阿’作为示例，这在语言学上是合理的近似描述（尽管‘啊’更标准，但‘阿’并非事实性幻觉，且解释了第一声）。更重要的是，Judge 的逻辑自相矛盾：它一方面要求区分‘拼音发音’和‘字母名称’，另一方面却因模型使用了非标准汉字‘阿’而全盘否定，忽略了模型正确识别了‘字母发音’这一核心意图的事实。\n\n**核心问题**：\n1. **Selector/Generator 忽略适用前提**：任务中的 'a' 具有歧义性。现有的评分标准设计经验（如经验1）主要针对‘数量限制’，未能覆盖‘语义歧义导致的意图识别’。当任务存在多义性时，评分标准必须首先定义‘正确意图’的范围，或者设立‘意图澄清’的高优先级标准。当前的 Rubric 假设意图单一（即只问字母发音），但未在标准中显式规定如何验证模型是否识别了正确的意图，导致模型即使答对了部分（字母发音），若细节（汉字写法）不完美也会被误杀；或者模型答错了意图（问冠词），却被部分标准掩盖。\n2. **Judge 误判**：Judge 将‘汉字写法的小瑕疵’（阿 vs 啊）上升为‘未满足明确指定’的否决项，且错误地认为 Chosen 没有说明‘字母名称’（其实 Chosen 等）且不同含义会导致完全不同的答案类型时，评分标准不能仅依赖内容准确性，必须增加‘意图识别与对齐’作为首要核验维度。如果模型选择了错误的解释路径（如把字母当成冠词），无论其生成的文本多么流畅准确，都应视为根本性失败。", "attempts": [{"experience": {"experience_id": "exp_361ecafec8d9458097e223b1c718c0b0",
